# The user interface

## 9. The user interface

### 9.1 Window

`Dashboard`: a 64-pixel rail (Watch, Project, Activity, Share, GPU — its icon carrying a badge where servers started from this app may still cost, and under its label two lines naming the one option a render will use (§9.7a) —, Settings, and the ? — `nav.help` — which opens the support dialog, `screens/support_dialog.dart`, anchored near the window's top and as tall as its tab: a *Get Support* tab — its introduction, off the web, saying that the recording can be described and sent; *Transcribe what I say* (`support.record.voice`, off the web); *Record an issue* (`support.record`), which starts the issue recorder (§6.8); and, off the web, the recordings not sent, each with *Send* (`support.unsent.<stamp>`) — an *About* tab with the app's name and the build's stamp (§6.6a), selectable, the specification's version and address and *Open-source notices* (`support.licenses`), and *Close* (`support.close`); while a recording runs the ? is a red stop button, disabled with a tooltip while one is starting or stopping, which off the web opens *Describe the problem* (§6.8), and, off the web, the rail shows the recording's microphone (`nav.record.mic`, §6.8), and while it listens a caption near the bottom of the window, beside the rail, shows what is being said; every picture the recording takes is marked by a brief flash over the window; Settings keeps its own *About* card; and beneath the ?, **the FilmOpen account's profile** — `nav.profile`, §6.6: a name and the account's state, which opens the account's popup — the rail scrolling, never cut, where the window is too short for all of it) beside an `IndexedStack` of eagerly built pages. The project's page keeps its layout while another of the rail's pages is shown, or while a route covers it — a dialog (`ModalRoute.isCurrentOf`) —, and takes the window's as it is shown again: a change of layout moves its detail pane's element, which the semantics cannot follow on a page the `IndexedStack` or a modal barrier hides from them. `NavIndex` numbers the pages — `project` 0, `activity` 1, `settings` 2, `watch` 3, `share` 4, `gpu` 5, numbers that never change — and the rail draws them in its own order; whatever reports a page names it (`NavIndex.nameOf`: `watch`, `project`, `activity`, `share`, `gpu`, `settings`), as the agent layer's `state`, the issue recorder's tape and a replay do. The app opens on Project. **Watch** (`screens/watch_page.dart`) holds two cards, *Tutorials* (`watch.tutorials`) and *Featured works* (`watch.featured`), each an empty state saying what will fill it and that it is a placeholder; it fetches nothing. **Activity** shows this device's usage first, then the media being added (§9.7); **Share** the open film's sends and receipts (§9.7); **GPU** the servers started for renders (§9.7a). Material 3, `ColorScheme.fromSeed`, compact density, light and dark themes, and an `AppColors` theme extension for the semantic colours the scheme lacks (the official star, a positive rating — also a plug-in that is ready —, the locked look, and `caution`, amber, for a plug-in that works less well than it could or whose turning off was not confirmed).

**The frame has two layouts on the left and two on the right** (`screens/dashboard.dart`). From **700 px of window** the rail stands at the left and, on Project, the tree beside the page, resizable. **Below 700 px** the rail and, on Project, the tree are **one drawer** (`SlideOut`, `filmopen_ui`) behind a menu button floating at the window's upper left (`nav.tree`): it slides in over the page, which the theme's scrim (`drawerTheme.scrimColor`: black at 0.4 in light, white at 0.3 in dark) tones toward neutral, its text and pictures with it, and the button becomes a back arrow that closes it. A section chosen in the drawer keeps it open, since the person moves between sections while it is open; a tree row chosen closes it, as a tap on the scrim and the system's back do. There is no tree outside Project. The drawer is as wide as the window less a hand's width, between the rail with the tree's minimum and 384 px, and the rail alone outside Project. It stays mounted while closed — moved out of view and out of the semantics, focus and taps — so that the rail's account profile keeps listening for the request that opens the account's popup; that request on a narrow window opens the drawer too (`nav.drawer {action: open, cause: hamburger|account}` and `{action: close, cause: hamburger|back|scrim|row|crossing, ms}`). **From `Breakpoints.assistantDocked` (1000 px of window)** the AI assistant is a full-height panel at the window's right, resizable from 280 px to 45 % of the window, its width remembered (`settingsProvider.assistantWidth`); **below it** its portrait floats at the upper right (`assistant.dock`, a button) and slides the same panel in from the right over the scrim (§9.3). Neither floating control has a row of its own: the first visible row of each screen clears them by the room `ChromeInsets` gives (`ChromeClearance`), at the start below 700 px and at the end below 1000 — the entity page's notices, stale-pick banner or header, the epoch overview, the project chooser and overview, the category, script, batch and other-files pages, Watch, Activity, Share, GPU's tab bar, Settings' title, its first card and the Plugins page's top — and nothing else moves. **The page keeps a floor** of `Breakpoints.pageMin` (360 px): the docked assistant gives way to it first, down to its minimum, then the tree, down to 220; the widths the person chose are kept and come back as the window grows (`SidebarSplitView.laidWidth`, the one rule). `layout.frame {wide, docked, clamped, cause: start|resize|section, window}` is logged at start and at each change of layout, with the widths laid out where the floor bites.

**The timeline dock** (`filmopen_timeline_ui`, §19.15). A scene's page — and no other — has its timeline docked beneath its tabs, across the pane: a handle (`timeline.handle`) to drag it up or tap it open and narrow, then one row; slid up, the tracks beneath that row. Its height is the person's, kept in the settings (`timelineHeight`) as the assistant's width is, and laid out between that one row (56 px) and half the page, so a stored height taller than half a smaller page is laid out at half and kept. Below 700 px of window it stays where it is, narrow by default, as the tree goes into its drawer. On Linux and Windows it plays the scene's picture and sound and exports its MP4 through MLT (§19.16); on the web and on mobile the fake's frame and block numbers stand in for the picture and play is disabled with its reason. Slid up, its picture takes the dock's height at 16:9, capped at half the dock's width, the tracks the rest: the person makes the picture large with the handle. **The pop-out** (`timeline.popout`, beside the export menu) opens the picture in a window of its own on Linux and Windows — a second view of the app's one engine, drawing the same texture as the dock, with its own transport and a full-screen button (`monitor.fullscreen`); space plays or pauses, F fills its screen or leaves it, Escape leaves full screen, then closes the window. The keys are the window's that has the keyboard: a key typed in the main window never reaches the pop-out, nor the reverse. The button closes the window again; elsewhere it is disabled, its tooltip saying why, and an open that fails is said in a message where it was asked. What the player refuses or fails is said in the pop-out too, in the dock's words, beneath its picture. Closing the main window closes the pop-out first, so the open drafts are written as at any exit.

**The browser page has two layouts** (`filmopen_browser/browser_page.dart`). At **700 px of window width and above** it is a `SidebarSplitView` (sidebar 220–560 px, seeded from settings, saved on drag end) with `TreePane` left and `DetailPane` right. **Below** it the detail pane has the page to itself, and the tree (`BrowserTree`, the `page.tree` region) is in the dashboard's drawer. Crossing the breakpoint keeps the detail pane's element — it is held by a `GlobalKey` — so a draft being typed is not written out from under the reader and the tab they are on survives the resize; the split is rebuilt rather than kept, because it is seeded once and would otherwise come back at whatever the layout last shrank it to.

**Tab order.** The rail, the tree and the page are each a Tab group of their own (`FocusTraversalGroup`, in `screens/dashboard.dart` and in both layouts of `filmopen_browser/browser_page.dart`): Tab walks one before it reaches the next, and reading order applies within each, so a page's fields are never interleaved with tree rows that happen to sit level with them. Within an entity's page the header, the tab bar and the body are groups of their own too (`filmopen_browser/entity_tabs.dart`), since the body scrolls on its own and a card scrolled under the header would otherwise share a line with the tabs. A scene's block card is a group of its own, walked in the order it is built (§9.3).

**The widths, and which axis each is on** (`filmopen_ui/theme.dart`'s `Breakpoints`). The drawer's 700 px and the docked assistant's 1000 px are measured on the **window**, because the side panels are what moves and the reader resizes a window; every other number is measured on the **pane** or the card that holds the thing, which the rail, the tree and the docked assistant beside it narrow, so a `LayoutBuilder` answers the second kind of question and `MediaQuery` the first. `assistantDocked` is never below the narrowest frame that docks uncrushed — the rail and its divider, the tree's 220, the assistant's 280, two 4 px splitters and the page's 360, 933 in all. Below **560 px of pane** a form puts one field on a row whatever width the field asked for; the scene grid gives up a column for every **300 px of pane** it lacks (`sceneCard`), from six down to one; below **300 px** a comparison gives away the page's padding and the cards', which is 72 px back into its two columns; below **240 px** it scrolls sideways at the width where it last fitted; below **260 px** a label sits above what it names rather than beside it — the version row's chips, the epoch chips and a nested attribute row; below **360 px** the tree's header puts the viewer's handle under the project's name (§9.2); below **360 px of row** *New project*'s *Browse…* shows its icon alone, its name its tooltip, so that the parent folder's box keeps room for a path (§9.2); below **360 px of card** a segmented button shows its labels alone, without the icons or the selected check, since every segment of one is as wide as the widest and three iconed ones want about 320 px that a phone's card has not — a `FittedBox` then scales the control rather than let a label break mid-word, which a `SegmentedButton` does in silence; and **260 px** is what a card in a grid of them asks for, which is a width rather than a threshold. The web build carries no `viewport` meta tag of its own, as Flutter's template does not: the engine writes one at start-up and warns on every load if it finds another.

**Heights matter too, and for one reason.** A header above an `Expanded` takes the height it asks for and leaves the body the remainder — and the headers here grow as they wrap, so at a narrow pane the remainder was nothing. Two of them now yield: the entity header above the tab bar takes what is left once the tabs have kept **240 px** and scrolls inside that, with a bar to say it is cut — keeping 80 px of its own on a page too short for both, so that it is cut rather than gone; and a comparison's column headings scroll with its rows once the page is under **240 px** tall, instead of standing above them.

**What a narrow pane costs, and what says so.** A `Row` reports that it overflowed; a `Wrap` does not, and the difference is not what it first appears. A `Wrap` **does** bound its children to its own width — a card that asks for 260 px inside a 215 px row is given 215 — so nothing paints outside. What it does instead is crush: at a 74 px row two chips that want 60 px each come out **two pixels wide, one to a line**, and no console line is printed. That is why the version row and the epoch chips put their label above their chips below 260 px rather than beside them: a 72 px label column beside a `Wrap` takes the last of its width, and what is left is unreadable rather than absent.

### 9.2 Tree pane

Header with project name and subtitle (folder path, or "bundled sample"), a chip showing the viewer handle when one is set — beside the name where the pane is at least 360 px wide and on a line under it where it is not, since a 120 px chip and three buttons beside the name left the name eight characters at the sidebar's 220 px minimum — expand-all / collapse-all in 32 px buttons rather than Material's 48, and the project menu (⋮): *New project…* and *Open project…* (on every platform; its dialog, below, offers the folder picker only where a folder dialog exists), *Close project* (drafts written, then a blank page saying *Start a new project or open an existing one*, absent while nothing is open, as are the two items after it), *Copy to a folder…* (for a session source without a disk root, wherever a folder can be named: the desktops, not a phone; copies every file, including media and thumbnails, and opens the complete copy), *Re-read files*, then a **Projects** section listing the bundled sample and every folder opened before (name, path — on a phone the path after the app's projects folder, `…/<tag>`, since every project's begins the same), the open one ticked. Opening any of them, or another folder, lets the project on screen finish its writes first (§7): where a draft cannot be written the project stays, its form showing why, and a message, kept for 10 s, says the other was not opened — with the refusal's own sentence, or, where the draft is still being written or failed without one, *See the page's save status, then try again*. *New project* says it inside its dialog, *Copy to a folder…* as it says a draft it waits for. Choosing a remembered folder that no longer exists removes it from the list with a snackbar, and the project on screen stays. Each remembered folder carries, at its right, an **×** that takes it off the list and leaves the folder alone, and — where folders can be made — a red **trash can** that opens *Delete this project*. The row, its × and its trash are three controls to a screen reader and an agent as to a pointer — `project.recent:<path>` opens the project, `project.recent-remove:<path>` and `project.recent-delete:<path>` are the buttons' —, the row being an entry of its own whose node holds neither button, where a menu item would merge all three into one node with a single tap. *Delete this project* asks one question naming the project folder and saying that it and everything inside it will be permanently deleted (`deleteProjectFolder(path)` in localization), with **No** (`delete-project.no`) and **Yes** (`delete-project.yes`) and no checkboxes. *No* or dismissing the dialog changes nothing. *Yes* checks `isDeletableProjectFolder` before closing or deleting anything: the target must be a real folder, not a link or filesystem root, with `filmopen-project.json` at its root; otherwise the app reports `projectDeleteNotAProject`. A project without the manifest still opens with a warning, but its trash action is refused before the open project is closed or any file is deleted. For the open project, identified by `sameFolderPath` (case and a trailing separator aside on Windows), drafts are written and the project closes before `deleteProjectFolder(project.path)` runs. Successful deletion removes its recent-project row and shows a message. A failure leaves the row and reopens what remains when the project was open. A separate media folder named by an existing manifest is always left alone; media inside the project folder is deleted with its contents. The loader reads media from the project's `media/` folder and ignores old manifest locators. Logged as `project.removedFromMenu`, `project.deleted`, `project.deleteRefused` or `project.deleteFailed`. Below the header, the filter field and the tree.

**New project dialog** (`filmopen_tree/new_project_dialog.dart`): the **title first**, with the focus; a little space; **Folder for the project:**; then, on a computer, the parent folder (typed or chosen with *Browse*) and the project's own folder name, **Folder** — the short name, in a `ShortNameField` that **follows the title** (`ShortName.normalise`: lower case, dashes for spaces, what the grammar does not allow dropped, cut to its length) **until the person types one of their own, and never afterwards**; a name typed first is left alone. The box says the format's rule only when it is broken, its typing dropping what the rule does not allow, and names a segment, never a path. Valid folder names receive no length advice, whether proposed from the title or typed by the person. Beside the parent folder, *Browse…* — its icon alone, its name the tooltip, where the row is under 360 px. **The parent folder is proposed** as the dialog opens (`newProjectParentProvider`, read afresh at each opening): the folder the last project was made in (`Settings.newProjectParent`), else, where none was saved, the folder that holds the last project opened (`parentFolderOf`; a drive's root kept as `C:\`, never the drive's current folder `C:`), else the person's documents folder as the operating system names it (`documentsDirectoryProvider`, `filmopen_platform`'s `documentsDirectory` through `path_provider`: Windows' *Documents*, the XDG documents directory on Linux, *Documents* on macOS) — either folder only where it still exists, a saved one since deleted giving way to the documents folder, never to the last opened project's. The answer may come late, and **never writes over a box the person has typed in or browsed into**; a documents folder the system will not name leaves the box empty and editable with *Browse*, and a line under it says so. The folder is remembered only once a project was made there and opened — never for a cancel, a refusal or a project not opened — and a write of it that fails stays for the session, said on Settings → General with *Try again* (§9.4). On a phone the parent row is absent: under *Folder for the project:* the dialog says the project goes into the app's projects folder of §6.4 (while the folder is being found *Create* waits and its tooltip says so, and a folder that cannot be made is a line under the form, `project.folderUnavailable` in the log, and the same tooltip). On the web the dialog says the project lives in the browser session only, and the short name's box is *Short name*, since no folder is made. **It asks no kind and no media location**, and shows no paragraph about folders and no preview of the files it will write: a new project has its manifest at the root, its project file and first scene in `data/`, and empty `media/` and `thumbnails/` folders, **written as the director** — the username in effect once the director's block has made it so (below), read after any change of it still waiting on an open draft has landed (`SettingsController.viewerSettled`).

**Open project dialog** (`filmopen_tree/open_project_dialog.dart`), from *Open project…* on every platform, in four sections. **Open a local folder**: *Choose a folder…* (`open.choose-folder`) where a folder dialog exists (`folderDialogsAvailableProvider`, §6.4), then the remembered folders (`open.recent:<path>`); a remembered folder that is gone comes off the list, as the menu's row does. **Your projects on {domain}**: signed out, *Sign in to {domain} to see your projects here* with **Login / Signup** (`open.login`), which closes the dialog — the account's popup is on the rail behind it — and asks for the popup with that sentence as its reason; signed in, the account's records (§6.6) grouped by username, each row (`open.record:<id>`) the project's name, its provider's mark (FilmOpen Cloud a cloud, Google Drive Drive's, a folder a folder) and its location; a `local` record whose folder this device has not, and a provider this build does not open (Dropbox, iCloud, any other word), are greyed with a tooltip saying why; a failed read says *Your projects could not be read ({code})* with *Retry* (`open.retry`), the local section still usable; no record, *No projects on {domain} yet.* The dialog follows the account's snapshot, so a sign-in that lands while it is open lists the projects. **Invited projects** and **Open an online project** are disabled, *Coming soon* under them, their tooltips what they will be (`open.invited`, `open.online`). Opening a record (§6.14): a remembered folder whose manifest names the record — FilmOpen Cloud by `sync.id`; Drive by the id of the folder the read-only lookup finds, with the project's tag, never by a folder's name, which Drive does not keep unique, so a Drive record asks Drive (and its sign-in) first, its folder on this device being opened offline from the local section — is opened; otherwise its manifest is downloaded into a new folder of the parent — the app's own folder on a phone, a folder the person picks on a computer — and that folder is opened. *Opening…* stands while it runs: *Cancel*, Back and the folder picker's wait hold the dialog, and the barrier never closes it, so an open under way ends in the dialog that started it. A local open — the picker, the folder's check, the open itself — ends every failure inline by a safe code. **An open is proven by the project, not by the call**: the project provider's state is read after `openDirectory`, and an error or another folder is said inline — *The project could not be opened ({code}).*, or the refused draft's own sentence (`open.failure`) — and logged as failed. It logs `project.openDialog {stage: shown | signIn | closed, signedIn, picker, remembered, records, outcome, ms on closing}`, `project.openDialog.records {stage: read | retry, outcome: start | read | refused | failed, count, local, code, status, type, error, ms}`, `project.openLocal.start | .done | .refused | .failed {how, path, code or stage: pick | check | open and type, cause, error, ms}` and `project.openRecord.start {provider, id, username, tag, location}`, `.step {stage: remembered | parent | lookup | manifest | bootstrap | open, outcome, code, ms}` and one terminal `.done | .refused | .failed {provider, tag, location, driveFolder, parent, stage, path, code or type, cause, error, ms}` (`error` on an open that loaded another folder names that folder) — a parent-folder picker cancelled is `.refused {stage: parent, code: noParent}` and says nothing; each remembered folder whose manifest cannot be read or parsed is logged, `project.openRecord.remembered {outcome: unreadable, stage: read | parse, path, reason: the type, error}`; every stage logs its start; a `local` record whose folder this device has opens as a remembered folder does (`how: record`).

**The downloading line.** Under the tree's header, while the open film's files are fetched from its target, *Downloading from {FilmOpen Cloud | Google Drive | the shared folder} — {n} files left* and a thin bar (`tree.downloading`): a fetch queued, working or waiting for a retry is left to do; the bar is the done fetches and the progress of those left over every fetch of the film in the sync's snapshot. A fetch waiting on a person — a sign-in, a pause, a form — needing attention or failed is blocked, and *{n} files could not be downloaded — see Share* is said under the files left and their bar, or alone where nothing is left; a cancelled one (a Stop) is neither, and the line goes when nothing is left or blocked. Media come down on demand (§6.14), so they are counted only once asked for. It logs `tree.downloading {film, state: active | blocked | done, left, blocked, total, ms}` at each change of a count or of its state, `ms` since the line began watching the film, the film as the first eight characters of its digest.

**The cloud and the director** (`filmopen_sharing`'s `CloudChoice`, `DirectorChoice` and the alias dialog, under the folder name). **Sync this project to the cloud so you can work on other devices and invite collaborators?** is checked by default; checked, four radios, each with its mark: *Use FilmOpen Cloud for $0.02 per GB per month (simplest)* with an ⓘ that opens a paragraph about it — enabled signed in to the account with a known balance above zero; signed out, *Login / Signup* under it; at a known zero, a red *You have no balance* and *Top up*, which opens the account page in the browser (a page that does not open says *The page could not be opened.*); a balance not known yet says so and is never taken for a zero. *Use your existing Google Drive account* — enabled where this machine is signed in to Google and the build has a Google client; signed out, *Sign in to Google* (the Sharing model's sign-in, its wait and *Cancel*; a sign-in that does not finish is said under it); without a client, the Sharing card's reason as the tooltip. *iCloud — coming soon* and *Dropbox — coming soon*, never enabled. A disabled radio's reason is its tooltip. The radio in effect is the one picked where it can take the project, else **the first enabled** (`CloudOptions.selected`). Checked with every radio disabled, *Create* is disabled with FilmOpen Cloud's reason as its tooltip; **unchecked, *Create* never waits on a cloud**. **Director role assigned to:** signed out, a username box (`ShortNameField(username: true)`) prefilled with the username in effect, `default` where none was chosen; an empty box is `default`, and an invalid one says the username's rule and leaves the last valid name in effect, so *Create* is never refused for want of a director; under it, *If you will ever collaborate or work on multiple devices:* and *Login / Signup*. Signed in, never typed: `AccountUsernameChoice` over the account's names (§6.6, read once per account, at sign-in), a pulldown, the primary first, the director selected where it is one of them, else the primary; its last item, *Add another username as an alias…*, opens the alias dialog, and a claim reads the names again. While they are read the director is shown read-only with a still progress mark; a failed read shows it read-only with *Your usernames could not be read ({code})* and *Retry*; an account with no username yet shows it read-only with *Add another username as an alias…*, which claims the account's primary — the director then kept may not be one of the account's. Both *Login / Signup* buttons ask the account's popup (`AccountOpener.open`, with its reason), which shows Settings; the dialog follows the account's snapshot and a source installed meanwhile, so that signing in at either removes both and shows the pulldown. **The alias dialog** (`showAliasDialog`): a username box checked with the website 500 ms after the last change (*Checking…*, *Available*, *That name is taken.*, *{domain} could not be reached.*, *The name could not be checked ({code}).*; an answer for a text typed over since is dropped); *OK*, enabled only for a name said to be available (its tooltip otherwise *Only a name shown as available can be claimed.*), claims it — the primary on an account with none, else an alias — and the pulldown selects it; a refusal is said inside the dialog by its code (*Your account has its three aliases already.*, *That name is taken.*, the username's rule, *{domain} could not be reached.*, else *The name could not be claimed ({code}).*), and *OK* then waits for a new check. **Create, in order**, with the director as *Create* was pressed — every stage uses that one, and while *Create* runs the cloud and director blocks take no pointer and no focus: the director becomes the username (`setViewerHandle`; a refusal is said in the dialog and nothing is made); the project is made as before; then, signed in — FilmOpen Cloud: `SharingModel.shareOnFilmOpenCloud`, which records the project itself; Drive: `recordProject` (provider `drive`, `FilmOpen/<tag>`) then `shareOnDrive`, which answers nothing — a notice says it failed, and the manifest's `sync` naming Drive that it went through (`shareNewProjectOnDrive`; neither is `notShared`); the box unchecked: `recordProject` (provider `local`, the folder's path; a project with no folder, the web's, records nothing) — and signed out, only Drive's `shareOnDrive`. Done, the dialog shows the Project page (`NavIndex.project`, which signing in from it had left on Settings) and answers the project. **A stage after the project that fails is the dialog's result**, never undone or tried again: *The project was created on this device.*, then each failed stage's sentence and the action that finishes it — a record not written (*Its record could not be written to {domain} ({code}). Settings → Sharing → Record on {domain}.*), a record whose confirmation this device could not keep (*The project is recorded on {domain}, but this device could not save that it is.*, or, for FilmOpen Cloud, *FilmOpen Cloud setup stopped: …*), FilmOpen Cloud not set up (*FilmOpen Cloud could not be set up ({code}). Settings → Sharing → Share on FilmOpen Cloud.*), Drive not set up (*Google Drive could not be set up ({code}). Settings → Sharing → Share on Google Drive.*, the Sharing model's notice code), an unexpected failure of either stage the same sentence with `failed` — and one button, *Open the project* (`new-project.open`), which lands as a create does; *Create* is not offered again and nothing dismisses the result. The log: `project.create.start {cloud, signedIn, director, directorSource, parent, tag, name, onDisk}`, `project.create.stage {stage}` before each of `director`, `create`, `record`, `sharing`, `project.create.director {changed, outcome, source, from, to}`, `project.create.created {project, scene, author}`, `project.create.record {provider, outcome, code, username, location}` (`skipped` for a project with no folder), `project.create.sharing {provider, outcome: ready | failed | notShared, stage?, code?}`, `project.create.stageFailed {stage, type, error}` for a failure nothing foresaw after the project exists, then one of `project.create.done {result: ready | partial, failures}`, `project.create.refused {stage, code, said}` or `project.create.failed {stage, type, error}`, each with `ms`; the choices' own `project.create.cloudChoice`, the account's names' `account.names` (§6.6), `project.create.directorChoice` (`{mode}` as the block's presentation changes, and `{director, refused?, source: field | pulldown, outcome: accepted | defaulted | keptLastValid}` (`refused` the invalid entry kept out) for each decision of the director, a typed one once the box is left rather than per keystroke), `project.create.signIn`, `project.create.topUp` and `project.create.driveSignIn` (each after its `.start`), `project.create.alias` (after `.alias.open`), and the alias dialog's `alias.check` and `alias.claim` (each after its `.start`), with `ms` on every outcome and the values each worked on — the username, the tag, the path — never a token. A disabled *Create* says why in its tooltip: the phone's folder, then the first box that is not good — **the reason names its box**: the empty parent folder on a computer (*Choose the folder the project goes into first*) or the folder's name (*Folder: …*, `ShortNameField.problemText`). A name that followed the title is checked as a typed one is (`ShortNameField` marks a value that arrives as touched), so a title cleared again leaves the box saying a name is required. The dialog's controls are `new-project.folder`, `new-project.browse`, `new-project.title`, `new-project.tag`, `new-project.cloud` (the box), the radios `new-project.cloud.filmopen`, `.drive`, `.icloud` and `.dropbox`, the buttons `new-project.cloud.sign-in`, `.top-up`, `.info`, `.drive-sign-in` and `.drive-cancel`, `new-project.director` and `new-project.director.sign-in`, `.alias` and `.retry`, `new-project.cancel`, `new-project.create`, the result's `new-project.result` (read-only) and `new-project.open`, and the alias dialog's `alias.username`, `alias.status` and `alias.refusal` (read-only), `alias.ok` and `alias.cancel`; the title and the folder name retain their dictation controls (§9.6). It refuses a non-empty folder with a message. **The new folder is opened only once it has been read back whole** (`ProjectController.createProject`): a folder written that will not list or load is `ProjectNotOpenedException`, said in the dialog with the folder it was written in — and that the project open behind the dialog stays open, where one is: after *Close project* none is — and the project on screen stays — its page, its viewer, no spinner. A failure said in the dialog goes once a box it was said of is changed. **While it creates, nothing dismisses the dialog** (a `PopScope`, *Cancel* disabled), since a dismissal would answer nothing while the project opens; a second press of *Create* while the first runs — a double click, Enter pressed twice — is ignored, so that the hold ends with the creation that set it and no other. *Create* answers a `CreatedProject` — the project file's stem and the first scene's — and the tree pane **opens the new project's own `pj` version on *Edit***, whatever tab the page before was on (the navigation asks its page for *View/Edit*, `BrowserController.navigate(onEdit:)`, since the tabs keep the reader's tab across pages where it stays open, as Compare does in development mode), its row highlighted and its ancestors expanded, the tree's filter cleared so that the row shows, the drawer closed below 700 px; the first scene, *Scene 1* in the person's language, waits in the script. The project's page has its Media card, so the landing writes the viewer's commentary file for the project on its first visit (§13.4).

**`ShortNameField`** (`filmopen_ui/short_name_field.dart`) is the one input box for anything that must be a segment (§5.2): it filters as the user types (lower case, spaces and underscores to dashes, other characters dropped), explains the remaining problem under the box, and says nothing of a valid name's length. With `allowWorkspace` it accepts one dot and validates both sides, for an author handle (§5.6): the owner a username of 3 to 32 characters, the workspace at most 20, counting up to 53. With `username` it is a person's username alone (`ShortName.validateUsername`), counting up to 32, its helper the username's rule where the caller gives none — *A username is 3 to 32 lowercase letters, digits and dashes, and does not start with a dash.* In either mode an owner too short, starting with a dash, of other characters or over 32 says that sentence; an over-long workspace names its 20. Any place that asks for a tag, an epoch token, a handle or a username reuses it.

**A row of the script has a menu** (`filmopen_tree/script_actions.dart`, `ScriptActions`): *Add folder…* and *Add scene…* (`tree.add-folder`, `tree.add-scene`) on the **Script** row and on every **folder** row — into the project file in use, or into the version of the folder the row draws — and *Remove from folder* (`tree.remove`; *Remove from the script* where the project file lists the row) on every row a list holds, a flagged entry that names nothing included, which is how a bad entry is cleaned out of a list from the app. The same identifiers on every row, one menu being open at a time. An item the viewer may not use is disabled with `PageAccess.childrenLock`'s reason as its tooltip. **Never a second write path**: each item calls the one `ProjectController` method for it, and the pages' Contents card calls the same `ScriptActions`. *Add* opens the **new-entity dialog** for a folder or a scene (`showNewChildDialog`): **the name first**, since the short name is filled in already — `nextSceneTag`, `nextFolderTag` (§5.11), overtypable, refused inline when taken — a folder's **kind** (the vocabulary and *None*), no username — the file is written as the username in effect, as a character's is — and no epoch question; a scene left unnamed is *Scene 3*, after its type and number — a numbered folder's `f` is dropped (*Folder 2*), and a short name a person typed is kept whole (*Scene flashback*). The short name's helper, which says why it is a number, may take as many lines as a phone's dialog needs (`ShortNameField.helperMaxLines`; a decoration's helper is cut at a number of lines, never wrapped freely). The identifiers stay `new-entity.*`. It settles the open drafts first: a draft pending on the parent's page lands before the parent is written again. On *Create* the new page opens, the drawer closes below 700 px as it does when a row is chosen, and a message says *Added …* — or, where the parent could not be changed, that the file is under *Not in the script* and why. *Remove* says that nothing was deleted and where the row went.

**Moving** (§3.10). A row whose folder or scene has no retained version — every one discarded — is for inspection only: it offers no script action and cannot be dragged, since a move would list what the reader then drops. A row whose entry names a folder or a scene also offers *Move up*, *Move down* (`tree.move-up`, `tree.move-down`; disabled at the ends with *It is the first already* / *the last already*, and with the lock's reason where the list is not the viewer's) and *Move to…* (`tree.move-to`); a row under *Not in the script*, which no list holds, offers *Move to…* alone, which files it — disabled, with the reason, where nothing of the script can be changed by anybody here (no username, a read-only project), rather than opening a dialog with nothing in it. An entry a reader cannot read stays where it is until it is repaired or removed. **Every move of one row is `ProjectController.moveChild` and nothing else** — the menu's items, the dialog and a drop (marked rows go together, below, through `moveChildren`): *within one parent* it is one write of that parent; *between two parents* **the destination is written first and the origin second**, so that a second write that fails leaves the entry listed twice — reported by every reader (`childDuplicate`), visible in both places, and said (*… is in … now, but could not be taken out of …, so it is listed twice: …*) — rather than nowhere; *from Not in the script* it is one write of the destination. **Refused before any write**: a folder into itself or into a folder beneath it (`folderIsOrHolds`, §5.12; `ContentProblem.folderIntoItself`), and, for **both** parents before either is written, everything an add is refused for — a draft that cannot be settled, a parent that is not the viewer's, a read-only project, a save the writer would refuse. An entry moves as it is written, so a pinned full reference stays pinned; a destination that names the folder or scene already takes no second listing, and the move is then the removal alone — **which is said** (*… was already in …: it was taken out of …*), since the spelling that was moved, a pin included, goes with the listing that was taken out; a move to where the row already is writes nothing; **nothing is deleted by any of it**. The open drafts are settled first, and a change of a list whose page is open goes through that page's draft, as an add's does. **A drop** (`ScriptActions.dragDrop`: `canDrag`, `placesOn`, `dropRefusal`, `drop`; the view's half is §8.3) **is that same move and nothing else**, so a drop writes what the same move by menu writes — a test compares the two folders' files. A row of the script can be picked up where its entry names a folder or a scene and the list that holds it is the viewer's to change, and a row beside the script whatever it is; a row that holds a list — *Script*, a folder drawn with what it holds, beside the script too — takes a drop **into** it, at its end, and a row a list of the script holds takes one **before** or **after** it, in that list, beside that row's entry as the move's turn finds the list (`ChildBeside`, read from the view, §7.5), never at the index the row was drawn at; *Move up* and *Move down* likewise move the entry one place from where that list then holds it. Refused, with the reason said on the drop: a list that is not the viewer's to change (the lock's own sentence), a folder into itself or beneath itself, and a row that is no place of the script (*Drop it on Script, on a folder, or between two rows of the script*). A refused drop is logged once, at the release (`tree.drop.refused`: the child and its list, the row and the place, the list it would go into, whether that list is locked and whether it would put a folder into itself, the reason and the time), and so is a drop whose row, or the row it was let go of over, the tree no longer holds at the release, or whose target no longer holds a list at that place (the same event, with `draggedGone` and `targetGone` in place of the lock and the cycle, and the code `childrenChanged` as its reason); a hover logs nothing. A *Move to…* whose chosen folder the film no longer holds when it is confirmed logs `tree.move.refused` (the child, both lists, `toGone`, the reason `childrenChanged` and the time); one whose opening row went while the dialog was open — a move published meanwhile, or the page changed under it — writes nothing and logs `tree.move.refused` with the reason `openerGone` and the time since the dialog opened; a cancel logs `tree.move.cancelled {child, from, ms}`, said by no message, since nothing it opened from is left to say it. Every refusal of a move past these is the controller's, logged as `folder.childMove.refused`. A drop into a folder says where the row went, since it may go out of sight; one between rows lands under the person's eyes. **The drop reads both rows as the tree has them now** (`TreeModel.nodeOf`): a drag carries the row it picked up, and a re-read under it would put the drop a place beside the line the person saw; a row that is gone is said (*The script changed meanwhile…*), not guessed. **The move dialog** (`filmopen_tree/move_dialog.dart`, `showMoveDialog`, `moveDestinations`) lists where the row may go: *Script*, and every folder the script draws **whose version the viewer may change**, in the tree's order and indented as the tree indents them — read off the tree's own model, so its rows are the tree's rows (`move.to:<the tree's row id>`) — **without the moved folder, wherever the script lists it, and what is beneath it, and without any folder the move itself would refuse** (`wouldCycle`, which is the controller's `folderIsOrHolds`, read on the film as the controller's decisions read it, `ProjectController.view` — a move of the app's own landed and not yet published counts, so a drop and the dialog refuse what the controller would; the dialog's rows are the tree built from that view when it opens, so a place such a move has made is offered too): a folder listed a second time is drawn without what it holds, so a folder the moved one holds can stand outside its branch as the tree draws it, and the dialog offers nothing that *Move* would then refuse; what was left out **because it is somebody else's** is said under the list — how many folders, and, since it is no folder, *Script* by name with the author of the project file in use — and with nowhere to go the dialog says what would give somewhere (*fork the project file, or a folder*). The file chosen is looked up again, as decisions read it now (the view), before anything is written to it. Choosing a row and pressing *Move* (`move.confirm`, disabled with *Choose where it goes first* until one is chosen; `move.cancel`) puts the entry **at the end** of the one chosen, and a message names where it went. The dialog writes nothing itself. **The messages** (`ScriptActions`): the project file's list is called *Script* in every one, as the tree's row, the menu's item and the dialog call it (`_listName`), and a folder's by its name; a message that says **what state the files are in** — a row now beside the script, a row listed twice, a destination that named it already, a refusal — stays ten seconds (`kLongMessage`, `filmopen_ui/messages.dart`), where a confirmation stays Material's four; and **a write development mode let through on somebody else's list is said** (§9.5): the tree has no page whose red line would say it, so the confirmation itself ends *This changed ann's version as bob: a release build would refuse it* — one message, since a second would take the first one's place before it was read, and **one string** (`guardOtherAuthorDone`, the confirmation its first placeholder), so that how the two sentences are joined is the language's to say — and a move within one list, which otherwise shows by itself, then has a message too.

**Moving marked rows** (§8.1, §8.3). A drag of a marked row, or *Move to…* in a marked row's menu, while more rows are marked, moves **every marked row** in one gesture: one call of `ProjectController.moveChildren` (§7) with the rows in the order the tree draws them — a folder, a row it holds and a row after it go as `[F, X, Y]`, never grouped by list — so one write per list touched and one publication of its own. An unmarked row, or a single mark, moves as above. **The rows, their lists and their order are read from the view** (`viewTreeModel`, the tree built from `ProjectController.view`, as the move dialog builds its rows): the ids drawn are only the person's choice, and a marked row the view no longer has where it was drawn — a move of the app's own landed and not yet published — is left where it is, counted in the log and in the message (*1 marked row had changed meanwhile and was left where it is*); whatever remains, one row included, goes through `moveChildren`. **Refused before anything is written**: a drop on, before or after a row that is itself marked (*Drop it on Script, on a folder…*: once the marked rows are taken out it has no place); a destination any marked folder is or holds (the folder-into-itself sentence); the lock as for one row; and the door's own refusals, an entry listed twice among them (*A row being moved, or the row it goes beside, appears twice in the script…*). A refused drop logs `tree.drop.refused` with the marked entries (`marked`), each by the list it comes from (`byOrigin`, as `tree.move.marked` gives it), whether the target is one of them (`targetMarked`) and which of them would hold the destination (`cycleWith`). *Move to…* on a marked row titles the dialog with their count (*Move 3 items to…*) and offers no folder any of them is or holds; its cancel logs the entries. **What is said**: *Moved 4 items to Act II*; nothing changed, *It is already there*; a list that could not give its rows up, *Moved to Act II, but Act I could not give up 2 of the rows, so they are listed twice: …*; written but not read back, the index's own sentence, after the half-done one where there is one; refused, *3 items could not be moved: …* — each a long message. The marks are cleared once the move is written, whole or not, unless a newer marking replaced them; a move refused whole leaves them. The gesture logs `tree.move.marked {count, entries, byOrigin, to, beside, door: drop|menu, skipped: {id: reason}, marked}` before the call and `tree.move.marked.done {outcome: moved|unchanged|halfDone|notPublished|refused, to, entries, landed, originsLanded, notRemoved, alreadyListed, code, error, reason, skipped, ms}` after it.

**Import Script…** appears beside *New project* in the project menu wherever projects can be created on
disk. Where a file can be chosen (the desktops) it opens the import dialog; where it cannot (a phone) it says
in a small dialog that a screenplay is imported in the desktop version only, with one *OK*
(`import-script.desktop-only`), and logs `project.scriptImportDesktopOnly {platform}`. The dialog has one
path box, a native *Browse* button offering one *Screenplay* group — `fdx`, `pdf`, `fountain`, `txt`, `rtf`,
`docx` and `fadein`, upper and lower case — *OK* and *Cancel*; the path box takes any file, since the file's
content decides its format (Project Specification §16.6). *Browse* logs `project.scriptImportPicker` with its
outcome — `started`, `selected` with the path, `cancelled` or `failed` with the error — and its `ms`; a failure
the controller does not answer with a code is logged as the error `project.scriptImportDialogFailed {path}`. A helper
names the parent folder, using the same proposal as *New project*. The store gives each imported project a
fresh folder there. A missing destination disables OK with a reason. A refusal stays inside the dialog,
retaining the path and the current film: a file that is not a recognised screenplay (`not_screenplay`) says
*FilmOpen could not recognise this file as a screenplay…*, a Final Draft file that cannot be read keeps a Final
Draft sentence, and every other code its own. During browsing or importing, repeated activation and dismissal
are held. Success closes the dialog, clears the tree filter and opens the imported project on Edit through the
browser's normal navigation. Identifiers are `project.import-script` and `import-script.path`, `.browse`,
`.ok`, `.cancel`.
The existing scene editor reads the imported dialogue, direction and action; source element types and
provenance remain available in JSON without adding another editor interaction.

### 9.3 Detail pane

Each offered reference-card job action has a `job:<id>:<action>` semantics identifier. Starting another action clears its previous local acknowledgement refusal; a rejected acknowledgement displays its code through localization again.

A form's saver is keyed by its rendered film and version, including both Compare columns. `OpenDraft.apply` accepts `reseed` and a `DraftWriteContext`: the latter belongs to the pending changed revision and stays with its snapshot through a failed save, a retry and disposal. An older in-flight snapshot keeps its original writer; incompatible pending contexts refuse before mutation. A successful snapshot releases its context only once every patch that shares it landed, leaving any later typing dirty by the existing generation rule. A bound writer's returned entity is the base of the draft's next write; a re-read is never offered to `adopt`. Discard releases the pending context. `reseed` compares the seed immediately before and after the mutation and updates only changed boxes synchronously, preserving unrelated text, caret and any later keystroke.

After the scene form writes a changed `epoch` or `cast` (compared canonically with the version before the write), it starts `createCastVersions` (§7.3) without awaiting it. The save succeeds independently of the companions and the helper never settles the saver's in-flight write. The film, viewer and controller are captured before the form can go away. A write changing neither field logs `scene.cast.versions.skipped` with the before/after values, reason `unchanged` and time. While the form is still mounted, helper completion shows its outcomes together: *Created {name} at {era}.*, or a localized refusal naming the character and era and why it could not be created. The era is its label, or its token where unlabelled; an ambiguous source says to choose a source in *Create*. A message containing any refusal stays for `kLongMessage` (ten seconds), including mixed creation/refusal outcomes; a creation-only confirmation stays four seconds. A disposed form shows nothing and writes no second scene save. `scene.cast.versions.shown` logs the scene, mounted state, outcomes, exact message, duration and its reason, and elapsed time, including completion after disposal.

Switches on the selection. When the project needs a choice, the **Project chooser** replaces everything: a card per `pj` candidate (name, stem, author, version, language, forked-from) that selects it.

- **The AI assistant's dock** (`AssistantDock`, `filmopen_assistant_ui`) stands at the window's right in every rail section — Watch, Project, Activity, Share, GPU and Settings (§9.1). From `Breakpoints.assistantDocked` of window it is a full-height panel (`assistant.dock`, a container); narrower, the active assistant's portrait floats at the upper right (`assistant.dock`, a button, hidden while the drawer is open) and slides the same body in from the right (`assistant.slideout`, closed by `assistant.hide` — a right arrow, as the left drawer closes with a left one —, the scrim or the system's back); the slide-out closes as the window docks (`assistant.panel {docked, width, cause}`, `assistant.panel {action: open|close, cause, ms}`). The body is three bands. At the top, frozen and at most 40 % of the panel's height, scrolling itself where the actions are more: the active assistant's portrait (`assistant.picker`), which opens a menu of the catalog assistants by portrait and name (`assistant.picker.<tag>`, an unavailable one disabled with its reason; choosing one leaves the chats held, each with the assistant it opened with), its name and where the person is (the held field's label, the page and the rail), and, while any chat is held, the switcher among them (`assistant.chats`, §6.11a); then its actions where the person is (`assistant.action.<id>`), one group per level in the catalog's order — the field's, its form section's, the page's section, the rail's, the always-offered — each headed by where it applies (the field's label, the form section's title as the form shows it, the page's name, the rail's), a heading the group above shows not repeated, a disabled chip carrying its reason: `noModel` only where the action has no candidate model at all, else the first candidate's refusal — `credentials` for a missing key, the offer carrying that provider's tag —, and `noTools` for a field's action whose model's provider declares no tools, since it could change nothing (*The model chosen for this action cannot make changes. Choose one that can in Settings.*); a provider no installed plug-in matched is never carried, and a diagnostic names a provider only as a safe word, else `invalid`. Under the actions, the first offer that lacks a key gives one readiness row (`assistant.readiness`): that sentence and *Add a key* (`assistant.addKey`), which closes the slide-out and opens Settings → Plugins on that provider's page, where its key card is (`assistant.dock {addKey, provider, ms}`, after the slide-out's close with `cause: addKey` on a narrow window). Where no offer lacks a key and one runs on FilmOpen credit, the same row says *Signed in, using FilmOpen credit.* instead. Under them, the conversation: *Starting assistant* and a refused open's reason, then the chat, which fills the rest of the panel; with no chat open it says what would fill it (`assistant.empty`). A chip opens the action's chat in the dock, beside the chats held, and nothing opens by itself; its first turn is under way at once, but for an action of id `ask`, the stock assistant's *Ask*, which waits for the person's question (§6.11a). **The AI icon on the form** (`assistant.shortcut.<id>`, §9.3) presses the chip: the form holds the place — the field, or the section's first field — and says the press (`assistant.shortcut {action, from: field|section, serial}`); the dock opens the slide-out where the panel is not docked (`assistant.panel {action: open, cause: shortcut}`), then chooses the active assistant's offer of that action where it is enabled, else refuses it with the reason, the chip saying why in the opened panel (`assistant.dock {shortcut, action, from, outcome: chosen|refused, reason?, opened, ms}`). A chip's own press is said the same way (`from: chip`) for the icon to hear, and not run again. The icon and the chip animate as one: the chip shows its selection, and the icon of that action pulses — out and back, 200 ms each way — on its own tap and on the chip's press, with no state beyond the running animation. The chat's X ends it, and a close begun elsewhere — a Discard on its record, its film left, the quit; another chip opens another chat beside it, and a move, another chip or another assistant ends none: the dock holds them, one shown, the others reached by the switcher (`assistant.chats`) or their rows in the tree's *Chats*, and a chat's first recorded change opens its record's page where the person is still where the chat was opened (§6.11a). Its voice conversation is offered on its own page alone — its project, rail, section and entity, whatever field is held and whichever version is shown —, disabled elsewhere with *Conversation is offered on the page this chat opened on*. The chat (`assistant.chat`) has an X fixed at its upper right (`assistant.close`, `assistant.dock {close, cause: x}` before its `session.ended`), which ends the chat and not the panel; the messages (`assistant.message.<n>`) in a transcript that scrolls through its history and follows new turns while the reader is at its end — the person's own scroll away stops it, their scroll back or their Send resumes it, and a change of the view's size keeps a following transcript at its end; then the reply box pinned at the bottom (`assistant.input`), one bordered box whose text runs its whole width from one line up to eight — fewer where the chat is short, never the room its X, its buttons, a busy line and an error as it wraps need —, then scrolls inside, and under the text, at the right, the microphone and **one button**: a square Stop while a turn runs (`assistant.cancel`, which cancels it), Send, an up arrow, with text in the box (`assistant.send`), the conversation with the box empty where the assistant and the device share a voice provider, whatever the text chat's state (`assistant.conversation`; the voice mode admits it itself), its request made at the press for the page as it is then — a field action's field always the chat's own: held where it is, else bound on the version the tabs show to that version's file as read, as a View form binds it, in place of any other field held (`assistant.dock {conversation.open, level, providers, field: none|held|bound|missing}`), else Send greyed with why; a box locked with text in it (the chat stale) keeps its text, Send disabled with its reason and no conversation. Each enabled press is logged, `assistant.composer.press {action: send|conversation|stop, state: busy|text|empty, text?}` (a Send's with the message sent), then its outcome once the session has answered it, `assistant.composer {action, outcome: sent|refused|stopped|opened, code, ms}` — a refusal with the session's code (`stale`, say), `ms` to that outcome, not the model's reply. A chat has no *Apply*: a change is written at once by the assistant's commands (§7.3). The transcript gives way first, down to nothing; where even a one-line box does not fit under the X, which stays fixed, the rest of the chat scrolls as a whole, held at its box, so the box is never cut. The microphone stands beside the box's text, not in it: a press focuses the box, and a click on it or on the one button is no click outside the box. The chat, and its unsent text, survive the slide-out's close and reopen and a change between docked and floating; a dictation under way in its reply box ends with the close — by its button, the scrim or the system's back — through the dictation's own stop, since a closed slide-out shows neither the capture nor its Stop (the panel's tickers are off while it is closed).
- **Field assistance** is a field action of the dock, served by the plug-in host's catalog service wherever the plug-in host runs; on the web the service is unavailable. A field keeps its dictation microphone; where the catalog in effect offers an action on the field itself, the AI icon (Material's outlined `auto_awesome`, the app's one AI mark) stands after the microphone as that action's shortcut, and where it offers one on the field's form section, the icon stands on the section's title row, after the badge and before the title (`assistant.shortcut.<id>`, the first action in catalog order where there are several, its tooltip the action's label from the package's strings) — only where the active assistant is available (its package without a refusal and its catalog loaded, the form building again as catalogs load); a field's icon stands in View as in Edit, with or without a microphone — after it where there is one, at its place where there is none (the prose editor draws a read-only box's suffix as its builder gives it) — and its press does what the chip's does, refused the same way, with the reason in the dock, where the chip is; a folded section has no title row for one. Where the slide-out covers the page (below the docked width), the chip's selection is the feedback, and the icon's pulse is seen where the panel is docked. The field's own door to the dock is unchanged: focusing a field in Edit, or tapping it in either mode, publishes it to the dock — the innermost field where one holds another (a prompt inside the media card): the prompt for a tap on or focus in its boxes, the media card for its own controls, though focus never left it — (its stem, pair id, apply path and draft — on a View form the entity as read, `FrozenAssistantDraft`, current while its file's text is unchanged), and Compare publishes nothing; building or focusing the field gathers no context and sends no request. A field's action is an ordinary chat that writes: it opens with the field's label and value, and the assistant changes the field with `update-entity` on the page's entity, as any change, into the person's draft — their own version labelled `ai: …`, which they pick when done — and the page opens the version written with the box at its check (below); the chat goes on, and a follow-up changes the draft in place. There is no Finish, proposal or *Apply* in a chat; the catalog's `finalize` is the voice conversation's alone. A changed viewer, project, model, package, grant or credential makes the chat stale; the field's form going, as its own write takes it away, does not.
**Credit in the dock and Settings.** A provider paid by FilmOpen credit is shown as ordinary information, without a missing-key warning. Readiness follows the selected action. A credit admission failure names the failed step and distinguishes an unreachable server, a refusal and no credit; earlier work stays in the conversation. With an empty composer, *Try again* sends *Continue from where you stopped.* through the ordinary send. A drafted correction keeps its words and can be sent normally. Credit waits continue until an answer, a transport failure or *Stop*; the elapsed seconds stay visible.

- **Synopsis voice** is a separate mode of a field's action, with a review of its own, the one the chat's conversation button opens on Windows, Android and Linux wherever the chat offers a conversation, a talk-only action's included. OpenAI uses WebRTC on Windows, Android and Linux; Grok retains its Windows WebSocket path. Settings can save keys normally. A catalog assistant is chosen in the dock's picker (`assistant.picker`); its chat's conversation then opens the voice mode. A successful package Check, a text-model choice or an OpenRouter key is not a voice prerequisite. The provider/model selector lists the routes the assistant's catalog and this device share, of those implemented here: Grok `grok-voice-think-fast-2.0` and OpenAI `gpt-realtime-2.1` on Windows, and OpenAI alone on Android and Linux. It preselects the Speech service saved in Settings → General (Grok where nothing is saved) where it is among them, else the first, and says so where the saved service has no route on this device; the text-model choice is not read. The host also refuses an unsupported route before reading context or acquiring resources. Opening the dialog is inert: what *Apply* would do is read from the host without a key, an operation or a peer — a talk-only action says nothing of *Apply* — and Start is offered once a route is chosen while the person is still at the chat's place; where the session Start opens would change another version than the one said (the pick moved meanwhile), nothing starts: its own sentence is shown and a second Start is asked for, and that sentence is shown thereafter. Start checks the admitted package and the funding, then begins one session and asks the opening question: on the person's own key for the chosen provider where one is saved (a malformed one refused as invalid), else on FilmOpen credit where the account is eligible — OpenAI over its WebRTC peer, a chosen Grok without its key running as OpenAI —, else refused for want of a key. Provider voice activity detection handles later spoken turns without Send. Listening, Thinking, Speaking, elapsed time and muted state are visible. Mute releases the input device, including during Connecting; Unmute resumes it. End stops live resources and preserves the transcript, and spends Finish, which then says the conversation has ended. Finish goes through the host's conversation: the drain, then the finalizer once where the drain finished, its outcome shown under `assistant.proposal` as *Was:* and the proposed text — the reviewed change's `before` and `after`, never a live read — with *Apply* (`assistant.apply`, `applyImportProposal` once per press) and *Discard*, or *The proposal changes nothing.*; closing while a proposal waits asks once — *Discard the proposal?*; a drain that timed out says the transcript is incomplete and nothing was proposed. Close releases resources before removing the dialog; while the finalizer runs after Finish, every way out waits for it, Close saying why. Errors and Finish feedback remain visible beside the controls, and the transcript follows new turns unless the person has scrolled back. Interrupted generated text is labelled as possibly unheard; OpenAI manages history truncation at its WebRTC output buffer, while Grok uses the measured local playback position.

On Linux an ordinary `flutter run -d linux` or Linux build, started from a graphical desktop, carries the voice mode. The bundled copy is on unless turned off and asks no consent (§6.11); a deliberate disable remains effective. Linux audio requires microphone, speaker and cleanup acceptance on the actual desktop.

Input captions appear as recognition arrives. Grok cumulative updates replace earlier words, while OpenAI live-caption fragments append within the provider's stable input item; a completed transcript replaces the partial text and cannot be overwritten by a late fragment. An empty input bubble says Listening, partial words are labelled as subject to change, and a stopped turn awaiting recognition says Finishing transcript. End retains unfinished words as a partial transcript, or says the transcript is unavailable when no words arrived. Muting marks cleared live input as partial; a later final can still replace it. An empty completed input also says the transcript is unavailable. Recognition completion is independent of the assistant starting to speak. The transcript follows updates only while the reader is near its bottom.

**Finish and the microphone owner in the dialog** (§6.11's session). Finish is the session's two-phase finish: the status reads *Finishing — waiting for the last words* with the elapsed time while input is stopped and the last captions drain (at most five seconds), Mute, End and Finish are disabled, and Close stays enabled and ends the session at once; then the status reads *Conversation ended*, Finish's outcome is shown, and a caption the drain did not complete is labelled *Partial transcript*. A Start the arbiter refuses, because a field's dictation or the recording's commentary holds the microphone, shows the sentence every microphone shares, naming the holder (*The microphone is in use by another box's dictation. Finish it, then try again.*); while the conversation holds it, a field's microphone and the commentary say *the AI assistant's conversation* has it (§9.6); since nothing was taken, Start may be tried again in place, and Finish is enabled only for a conversation that began. The provider pull-down preselects the Speech service's effective route (§9.6; Grok where nothing is chosen) where this device offers it, and OpenAI — with a line naming the service and where it is chosen — where the chosen service is unknown or has no route here; it writes nothing back. A Grok the host starts as OpenAI on credit (§6.11a) shows OpenAI in the pull-down and OpenAI's billing line from then on.

The voice mode has no cap of the app's on a conversation: it runs until the person finishes or ends it, or the provider or the connection ends it, and the dialog names no limit; a new conversation is a new Start, never automatic, and no allowance accumulates across conversations. Only an explicit Start gesture may create a paid conversation; a failed attempt never retries itself. Key and model access remain unchecked until Start, and the dialog says so. A missing key is saved through the normal FilmOpen Settings using the usual native credential store; Start refused by the key's store says which state it found, in the sentences already in use — *The speech service you chose has no key yet…* for a missing record, *The key saved for this service is not valid…* for a malformed one, *The key couldn't be read from this device…* for a store it cannot read. OpenAI input captions use `gpt-live-transcribe` within the WebRTC call; incoming deltas and the final transcript share the provider's item identity. Caption errors stop the session. There is no separate OpenAI transcription socket. Usage retains reported units and labelled cost estimates (§6.7), without a price prompt. FilmOpen-funded voice is unavailable in this build. A local timer does not establish a provider charge. This mode creates no character and runs no finalizer of its own accord: its Finish writes no project file, and only *Apply* writes.

- **Use the LLM:** is the one machine model override in Settings → General, independent of Dictation's speech service. Its first enabled entry, *Let the AI assistant decide*, is selected by default and removes `assistant.textModel`. Every text model offered by enabled providers follows once, local models included, named by the catalogue; usable models are enabled, unavailable ones disabled with their reason. A saved model no longer offered remains visible with its reason so it can be cleared. The menu (`assistant.textModel`) stays within the field's width and a fixed scrolling height. Choosing a model stores its id under the same key; an old saved route is read by its model alone. Serialized select and clear writes retain the newest choice across restart. The choice overrides plug-in preferences for future LLM calls; current conversations keep their captured decision. `assistant.textModel.select.start`/`.select` and `.clear.start`/`.clear` log the old and new values, decision reason, outcome, applied state and elapsed time; a failure includes its stage, type and message. Inventory logs its reasons and trials only on change through `assistant.model` and `assistant.models`, without call-decision lines; an unavailable model asks for a provider key, credit or a local server. Model resolution and assistant route admission are §6.11/§6.11a's.

- **Project landing** — opening a source shows the selected project version on its ordinary entity page. A folder needing a project choice asks which version to open; choosing one opens that exact version. A read-only session choice writes no commentary file; a writable choice with a viewer records its pick. Navigating to the project landing returns to the version in use and keeps following it when the viewer changes; an explicitly selected stem remains pinned. Its View/Edit scroll begins with **Warnings**, folded initially and showing the complete folder diagnostic count; opening it lists every diagnostic in the current language, including on a read-only project page. Zero warnings reads *Nothing to report.* A folder without a selected project version has a minimal landing with those diagnostics visible without a fold, its first card clearing the floating chrome. There is no separate project summary: General and Synopsis hold the title, kind, genres and text; Epochs holds the declarations and their editing; Other holds Release, the file attributes, contributors and tracks through the uncovered-attribute rule. Category navigation stays in the tree and library configuration in Settings. The warnings' gesture logs `project.warnings.fold.start` and `project.warnings.fold` with old/new state, full diagnostic text, count, reason, outcome and elapsed time.
- **Script page** (`filmopen_browser/script_page.dart`, `ScriptSelection`) — a title with the count over three tabs, **Contents**, **Script** and **Workflow**: the entity pages' own tabs (`EntityTabs`), so the tab shown is told to the assistant as an entity page's is (§6.11a), and an agent opens them by name, `contents`, `script` and `workflow`. **Script** is the whole film read as a folder's Script tab reads it (below). **Workflow** is the chosen scriptwriting plug-in's steps (below) under the **Create Script** card (`filmopen_browser/workflow_tab.dart`): a pull-down **Scriptwriting plug-in** (`workflow.plugin`) — *None*, then each enabled plug-in that declares a workflow, by its name, in the host's order, those whose workflow was refused included; no plug-in is chosen for the person, and a chosen one no longer enabled stays chosen as its tag, the tab saying it is not installed or is turned off — and a box **Stop a step at USD** (`workflow.ceiling`, *No limit* while empty), kept as it is typed, so that leaving the page by any door — the tree, another tab, Enter or none — loses nothing: empty is no limit, an amount above 0 is kept (through the preferences' own lines; an amount equal to the one kept writes nothing), and anything else — 0, a negative amount, words, a decimal comma — is said under the box (*Enter an amount above 0, like 2.50, or leave it empty for no limit.*) and nothing is kept, words logged `workflow.ceiling.unread`, a number not above 0 refused by the preferences with its reason. The two are this computer's preferences (`workflowPreferencesProvider`, §7); on a phone's width they go one to a row. The plug-ins are read when the tab opens and again whenever the host's plug-ins change; in place of the steps the tab says what would fill it: no scriptwriting plug-in enabled, none chosen, the chosen one missing, its workflow refused (with the reader's code and where), or the read failing (with the error, logged `workflow.tab.failed` with the stage, the plug-in, the focus and the time). **Contents** is the script as a file manager shows a folder beside the tree: a **Contents** card (`contents_card.dart`) of what the project file in use holds, in order — each row an icon, the name, the detail and the warning where one applies, **in the tree's own words** (`ScriptRowText`), opening its page on a tap — with **Add folder…** and **Add scene…** over the rows (`contents.add-folder`, `contents.add-scene`) and a ⋯ on each row (`contents:<entry>`, its menu `contents:<entry>.menu` with `contents.remove`; a second equal entry's identifier carries `#2`, and a `%`, a `/` or a `#` the entry itself writes is written as its code, `%25`, `%2F` or `%23` — the tree's rule, `scriptRowIdText` —, so that `sc_1#2` as written is never the second `sc_1`). An empty script says what would fill it (`EmptyState`: *Nothing in the script yet — add a scene, or a folder to keep scenes in*), and **an empty card that cannot be added to says why under that sentence** (`EmptyState.detail`, the lock's reason): the buttons' tooltips need a hover, which a phone has not. A second card, **Not in the script**, lists what the tree's row of that name lists and is absent when nothing is unfiled; its rows are flagged, as the tree's are, and their menu's *Move to…* files the entry, which no list holds. **The two cards' rows are marked as the tree's are, as a file manager marks** (§8.1): a **mouse** click marks the row and makes it the anchor without leaving the page, shift-click marks the range over both cards' rows as drawn — Script's, then *Not in the script*'s, never a folder's own contents — and control- or command-click adds or takes away; a second plain click on the same row within the double-click time, nothing clicked between, opens its page, timed from the pointer's own events so a single click is never delayed; a **touch**, and a tap with no pointer (a screen reader's, an agent's), opens it as before. A marked row is tinted as the tree's, and *Move to…* in a marked row's menu moves every marked row (§9.2); the Contents has no drag. The marks' ids are the tree's (`scriptRowIds`), the `contents:<entry>` identifiers unchanged; a row's opening logs `contents.row.opened {row, mark, by, outcome}`. A folder's page's Contents is not marked. Both cards read `scriptOutlineProvider`'s walk, never one of the page's own. **The card is never a second write path**: its buttons and its menu are `ScriptActions` (§9.2), the same object the tree's menu calls, and on a version that is not the viewer's the buttons are disabled with `PageAccess.childrenLock`'s reason as their tooltip — inside the button's `Semantics(identifier:)`, so that the reason is the button's own in the semantics tree and not the card's. An add into a version of the viewer's own that the script does not draw — an older one, chosen in the version pull-down — says so (*…a version of … that is not the one in use, so it is under Not in the script*), since the new row then turns up beside the script.
- **A folder's page** is the entity page every type has — the header and the version row, *View/Edit* and *Compare*. On *View/Edit* it shows the same **Contents** card, for the version on screen, **above the form** (`EntityDataTab.leading`, `EntityForm.leading`: the card is the pages' and the form the forms', which may import neither the tree nor the pages, so the page hands the card in as a widget), then the folder's form through the one type door (`folder_edit_tab.dart`): **Name**; **Kind**, the vocabulary as chips (`pathChoiceField` over `folderKinds`, a token the vocabulary does not know shown as a chosen chip of its own, as written — chips and not a pull-down, the kind being a word the app makes nothing of, which a line under them says: *A word for what the folder is. It decides nothing; tap the chosen one to have none*, `pathChoiceField`'s `helper`); **Synopsis** and **Notes**, multi-line; its pictures (the media card, *Reference images*) and its **visual treatment** (below); then the plug-ins' cards for `fo` (`pluginSectionsFor`), then *Other attributes*. `children` is drawn once on the tab — as the card (`hiddenKeys`) — and as one row in Compare, where copy-left moves the list as it moves any list. The card's rows are `contentsOf(outline, project, version)`: the script's own rows for that version where the script draws it, so that a second listing in another list is flagged here as in the tree, else the file's list alone (`childRowsOf`) — another author's version on its own page. The form's trailing *Notes* card is not drawn where a field has the key.
